But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I don't get stuck on specific images or anything like that. It's really just... the worrying. Like my mind is just racing with things I need to do or things that could go wrong, mostly with my team or the sales numbers. But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I mean, I definitely feel run down and tired all the time from not sleeping, but I haven't noticed any actual physical pain or sickness like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about work or sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with work or your sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, work is definitely a big part of it, but my mind just kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Most days, I'll start out stressing over whether we'll hit our launch numbers or if someone on my team is about to quit. But then it just shifts right over to personal things, like paying the mortgage, and then back to a client renewal. \n\nIt just bounces around all day. It honestly feels like every small delay, no matter what it's about, could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're alone with no specific tasks or responsibilities to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Even when I have absolutely nothing on my plate, my brain just doesn't stop. I can literally tell myself, \"Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go.\" But... I don't know, five minutes later I'm right back in another loop. It's like I check one concern off in my head, only to have three more pop up. I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I mean, I definitely feel run down and tired all the time from not sleeping, but I haven't noticed any actual physical pain or sickness like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about work or sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with work or your sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, work is definitely a big part of it, but my mind just kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Most days, I'll start out stressing over whether we'll hit our launch numbers or if someone on my team is about to quit. But then it just shifts right over to personal things, like paying the mortgage, and then back to a client renewal. \n\nIt just bounces around all day. It honestly feels like every small delay, no matter what it's about, could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're alone with no specific tasks or responsibilities to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Even when I have absolutely nothing on my plate, my brain just doesn't stop. I can literally tell myself, \"Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go.\" But... I don't know, five minutes later I'm right back in another loop. It's like I check one concern off in my head, only to have three more pop up. I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety always related to something specific that's happening or might happen, or do you ever feel anxious without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It's usually tied to something. I mean, there's always a reason I can point to, even if it's just a bunch of small things piling up. I don't really just sit there feeling anxious out of nowhere... my mind is always working on a specific problem or worrying about a scenario that might happen. It's just that there are so many of them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety seem to follow any kind of pattern or cycle throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a specific pattern, to be honest. It doesn't really cycle or peak at a certain time... it's just kind of always there. Like I mentioned, it's just this constant background noise from the time I wake up until I go to bed. So, no, I wouldn't say it follows any sort of schedule.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it seem to come on without any trigger at all?\n\nPatient: It's definitely triggered by specific things, but there are just so many of them. Most days, my mind just jumps from one issue to the next. Like, I'll start out worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers, and then I'm suddenly stressing about whether someone on my sales team is about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like the mortgage, or back to work with a client renewal. \n\nIt just feels like... I don't know, like every small delay or hiccup could turn into something major. So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um, I'm mainly here because my sleep has gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nIt's just... even when I go to bed completely exhausted, I just lie there replaying sales forecasts and team issues from work. And then I'll usually wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and I just can't get my brain to power down again after that.\n\nDoctor: How much are these sleep issues and thoughts about work affecting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely starting to affect things. Both at work and at home, really. I mean, at work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that our pipeline reviews end up running pretty tense lately. And then... by the time I finally get home, I'm just completely drained. I end up being really reactive, and I'm just not fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or mental images that you can't control, outside of just worrying about work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't get stuck on specific images or anything like that. It's really just... the worrying. Like my mind is just racing with things I need to do or things that could go wrong, mostly with my team or the sales numbers. But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I mean, I definitely feel run down and tired all the time from not sleeping, but I haven't noticed any actual physical pain or sickness like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about work or sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with work or your sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, work is definitely a big part of it, but my mind just kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Most days, I'll start out stressing over whether we'll hit our launch numbers or if someone on my team is about to quit. But then it just shifts right over to personal things, like paying the mortgage, and then back to a client renewal. \n\nIt just bounces around all day. It honestly feels like every small delay, no matter what it's about, could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're alone with no specific tasks or responsibilities to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Even when I have absolutely nothing on my plate, my brain just doesn't stop. I can literally tell myself, \"Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go.\" But... I don't know, five minutes later I'm right back in another loop. It's like I check one concern off in my head, only to have three more pop up. I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety always related to something specific that's happening or might happen, or do you ever feel anxious without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It's usually tied to something. I mean, there's always a reason I can point to, even if it's just a bunch of small things piling up. I don't really just sit there feeling anxious out of nowhere... my mind is always working on a specific problem or worrying about a scenario that might happen. It's just that there are so many of them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety seem to follow any kind of pattern or cycle throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a specific pattern, to be honest. It doesn't really cycle or peak at a certain time... it's just kind of always there. Like I mentioned, it's just this constant background noise from the time I wake up until I go to bed. So, no, I wouldn't say it follows any sort of schedule.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it seem to come on without any trigger at all?\n\nPatient: It's definitely triggered by specific things, but there are just so many of them. Most days, my mind just jumps from one issue to the next. Like, I'll start out worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers, and then I'm suddenly stressing about whether someone on my sales team is about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like the mortgage, or back to work with a client renewal. \n\nIt just feels like... I don't know, like every small delay or hiccup could turn into something major. So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related issues or does it also cover a wide range of other areas of your life equally?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just work. Work is a big part of it, sure, because there's so much going on there, but it spills over into everything else, too. \n\nLike I said, I'll worry about the mortgage, or if my car makes a weird noise I start stressing about how much it'll cost to fix and if we can afford it. I worry about my family, my health... just everyday things. It really just jumps around to whatever is going on in my life at the time. So, yeah, it covers a pretty wide range.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety always related to something specific that's happening or might happen, or do you ever feel anxious without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It's usually tied to something. I mean, there's always a reason I can point to, even if it's just a bunch of small things piling up. I don't really just sit there feeling anxious out of nowhere... my mind is always working on a specific problem or worrying about a scenario that might happen. It's just that there are so many of them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety seem to follow any kind of pattern or cycle throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a specific pattern, to be honest. It doesn't really cycle or peak at a certain time... it's just kind of always there. Like I mentioned, it's just this constant background noise from the time I wake up until I go to bed. So, no, I wouldn't say it follows any sort of schedule.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it seem to come on without any trigger at all?\n\nPatient: It's definitely triggered by specific things, but there are just so many of them. Most days, my mind just jumps from one issue to the next. Like, I'll start out worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers, and then I'm suddenly stressing about whether someone on my sales team is about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like the mortgage, or back to work with a client renewal. \n\nIt just feels like... I don't know, like every small delay or hiccup could turn into something major. So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related issues or does it also cover a wide range of other areas of your life equally?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just work. Work is a big part of it, sure, because there's so much going on there, but it spills over into everything else, too. \n\nLike I said, I'll worry about the mortgage, or if my car makes a weird noise I start stressing about how much it'll cost to fix and if we can afford it. I worry about my family, my health... just everyday things. It really just jumps around to whatever is going on in my life at the time. So, yeah, it covers a pretty wide range.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel your anxiety is significantly less than in others?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really. I wish I could say there was some part of my life where I could just turn it off, but it kind of bleeds into everything. Even when I'm supposed to be relaxing on the weekend or doing something fun, my brain is usually still going over some list of things I need to handle or worrying about what's coming up next. So, no, I haven't really found any area where it's noticeably less.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um, I'm mainly here because my sleep has gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nIt's just... even when I go to bed completely exhausted, I just lie there replaying sales forecasts and team issues from work. And then I'll usually wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and I just can't get my brain to power down again after that.\n\nDoctor: How much are these sleep issues and thoughts about work affecting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely starting to affect things. Both at work and at home, really. I mean, at work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that our pipeline reviews end up running pretty tense lately. And then... by the time I finally get home, I'm just completely drained. I end up being really reactive, and I'm just not fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or mental images that you can't control, outside of just worrying about work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't get stuck on specific images or anything like that. It's really just... the worrying. Like my mind is just racing with things I need to do or things that could go wrong, mostly with my team or the sales numbers. But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um, I'm mainly here because my sleep has gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nIt's just... even when I go to bed completely exhausted, I just lie there replaying sales forecasts and team issues from work. And then I'll usually wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and I just can't get my brain to power down again after that.\n\nDoctor: How much are these sleep issues and thoughts about work affecting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely starting to affect things. Both at work and at home, really. I mean, at work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that our pipeline reviews end up running pretty tense lately. And then... by the time I finally get home, I'm just completely drained. I end up being really reactive, and I'm just not fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or mental images that you can't control, outside of just worrying about work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't get stuck on specific images or anything like that. It's really just... the worrying. Like my mind is just racing with things I need to do or things that could go wrong, mostly with my team or the sales numbers. But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I mean, I definitely feel run down and tired all the time from not sleeping, but I haven't noticed any actual physical pain or sickness like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about work or sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with work or your sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, work is definitely a big part of it, but my mind just kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Most days, I'll start out stressing over whether we'll hit our launch numbers or if someone on my team is about to quit. But then it just shifts right over to personal things, like paying the mortgage, and then back to a client renewal. \n\nIt just bounces around all day. It honestly feels like every small delay, no matter what it's about, could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're alone with no specific tasks or responsibilities to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Even when I have absolutely nothing on my plate, my brain just doesn't stop. I can literally tell myself, \"Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go.\" But... I don't know, five minutes later I'm right back in another loop. It's like I check one concern off in my head, only to have three more pop up. I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety always related to something specific that's happening or might happen, or do you ever feel anxious without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It's usually tied to something. I mean, there's always a reason I can point to, even if it's just a bunch of small things piling up. I don't really just sit there feeling anxious out of nowhere... my mind is always working on a specific problem or worrying about a scenario that might happen. It's just that there are so many of them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety seem to follow any kind of pattern or cycle throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a specific pattern, to be honest. It doesn't really cycle or peak at a certain time... it's just kind of always there. Like I mentioned, it's just this constant background noise from the time I wake up until I go to bed. So, no, I wouldn't say it follows any sort of schedule.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it seem to come on without any trigger at all?\n\nPatient: It's definitely triggered by specific things, but there are just so many of them. Most days, my mind just jumps from one issue to the next. Like, I'll start out worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers, and then I'm suddenly stressing about whether someone on my sales team is about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like the mortgage, or back to work with a client renewal. \n\nIt just feels like... I don't know, like every small delay or hiccup could turn into something major. So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related issues or does it also cover a wide range of other areas of your life equally?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just work. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um, I'm mainly here because my sleep has gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nIt's just... even when I go to bed completely exhausted, I just lie there replaying sales forecasts and team issues from work. And then I'll usually wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and I just can't get my brain to power down again after that.\n\nDoctor: How much are these sleep issues and thoughts about work affecting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely starting to affect things. Both at work and at home, really. I mean, at work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that our pipeline reviews end up running pretty tense lately. And then... by the time I finally get home, I'm just completely drained. I end up being really reactive, and I'm just not fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or mental images that you can't control, outside of just worrying about work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't get stuck on specific images or anything like that. It's really just... the worrying. Like my mind is just racing with things I need to do or things that could go wrong, mostly with my team or the sales numbers. But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I mean, I definitely feel run down and tired all the time from not sleeping, but I haven't noticed any actual physical pain or sickness like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about work or sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with work or your sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, work is definitely a big part of it, but my mind just kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Most days, I'll start out stressing over whether we'll hit our launch numbers or if someone on my team is about to quit. But then it just shifts right over to personal things, like paying the mortgage, and then back to a client renewal. \n\nIt just bounces around all day. It honestly feels like every small delay, no matter what it's about, could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're alone with no specific tasks or responsibilities to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Even when I have absolutely nothing on my plate, my brain just doesn't stop. I can literally tell myself, \"Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go.\" But... I don't know, five minutes later I'm right back in another loop. It's like I check one concern off in my head, only to have three more pop up. I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety always related to something specific that's happening or might happen, or do you ever feel anxious without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It's usually tied to something. I mean, there's always a reason I can point to, even if it's just a bunch of small things piling up. I don't really just sit there feeling anxious out of nowhere... my mind is always working on a specific problem or worrying about a scenario that might happen. It's just that there are so many of them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety seem to follow any kind of pattern or cycle throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a specific pattern, to be honest. It doesn't really cycle or peak at a certain time... it's just kind of always there. Like I mentioned, it's just this constant background noise from the time I wake up until I go to bed. So, no, I wouldn't say it follows any sort of schedule.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it seem to come on without any trigger at all?\n\nPatient: It's definitely triggered by specific things, but there are just so many of them. Most days, my mind just jumps from one issue to the next. Like, I'll start out worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers, and then I'm suddenly stressing about whether someone on my sales team is about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like the mortgage, or back to work with a client renewal. \n\nIt just feels like... I don't know, like every small delay or hiccup could turn into something major. So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related issues or does it also cover a wide range of other areas of your life equally?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just work. Work is a big part of it, sure, because there's so much going on there, but it spills over into everything else, too. \n\nLike I said, I'll worry about the mortgage, or if my car makes a weird noise I start stressing about how much it'll cost to fix and if we can afford it. I worry about my family, my health... just everyday things. It really just jumps around to whatever is going on in my life at the time. So, yeah, it covers a pretty wide range.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel your anxiety is significantly less than in others?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um, I'm mainly here because my sleep has gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nIt's just... even when I go to bed completely exhausted, I just lie there replaying sales forecasts and team issues from work. And then I'll usually wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and I just can't get my brain to power down again after that.\n\nDoctor: How much are these sleep issues and thoughts about work affecting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely starting to affect things. Both at work and at home, really. I mean, at work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that our pipeline reviews end up running pretty tense lately. And then... by the time I finally get home, I'm just completely drained. I end up being really reactive, and I'm just not fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or mental images that you can't control, outside of just worrying about work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't get stuck on specific images or anything like that. It's really just... the worrying. Like my mind is just racing with things I need to do or things that could go wrong, mostly with my team or the sales numbers. But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I mean, I definitely feel run down and tired all the time from not sleeping, but I haven't noticed any actual physical pain or sickness like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about work or sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with work or your sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, work is definitely a big part of it, but my mind just kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Most days, I'll start out stressing over whether we'll hit our launch numbers or if someone on my team is about to quit. But then it just shifts right over to personal things, like paying the mortgage, and then back to a client renewal. \n\nIt just bounces around all day. It honestly feels like every small delay, no matter what it's about, could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're alone with no specific tasks or responsibilities to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Even when I have absolutely nothing on my plate, my brain just doesn't stop. I can literally tell myself, \"Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go.\" But... I don't know, five minutes later I'm right back in another loop. It's like I check one concern off in my head, only to have three more pop up. I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety always related to something specific that's happening or might happen, or do you ever feel anxious without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It's usually tied to something. I mean, there's always a reason I can point to, even if it's just a bunch of small things piling up. I don't really just sit there feeling anxious out of nowhere... my mind is always working on a specific problem or worrying about a scenario that might happen. It's just that there are so many of them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety seem to follow any kind of pattern or cycle throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a specific pattern, to be honest. It doesn't really cycle or peak at a certain time... it's just kind of always there. Like I mentioned, it's just this constant background noise from the time I wake up until I go to bed. So, no, I wouldn't say it follows any sort of schedule.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it seem to come on without any trigger at all?\n\nPatient: It's definitely triggered by specific things, but there are just so many of them. Most days, my mind just jumps from one issue to the next. Like, I'll start out worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers, and then I'm suddenly stressing about whether someone on my sales team is about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like the mortgage, or back to work with a client renewal. \n\nIt just feels like... I don't know, like every small delay or hiccup could turn into something major. So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related issues or does it also cover a wide range of other areas of your life equally?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just work. Work is a big part of it, sure, because there's so much going on there, but it spills over into everything else, too. \n\nLike I said, I'll worry about the mortgage, or if my car makes a weird noise I start stressing about how much it'll cost to fix and if we can afford it. I worry about my family, my health... just everyday things. It really just jumps around to whatever is going on in my life at the time. So, yeah, it covers a pretty wide range.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel your anxiety is significantly less than in others?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really. I wish I could say there was some part of my life where I could just turn it off, but it kind of bleeds into everything. Even when I'm supposed to be relaxing on the weekend or doing something fun, my brain is usually still going over some list of things I need to handle or worrying about what's coming up next. So, no, I haven't really found any area where it's noticeably less.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is something that you experience in response to specific situations, or is it more of a general feeling that you have all the time, regardless of what's going on?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more of a general feeling that just stays with me all the time. I mean, I feel wound up almost constantly. It's like... it's like I'm sitting in a stressful pipeline review at work, but I'm actually just at home eating dinner. And physically, my shoulders just stay tight no matter how much I try to relax. So even if there isn't a specific crisis happening right in front of me, that tense feeling is still just there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off from work or cancel plans due to feeling overwhelmed by your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I haven't actually taken a full day off work, no. But... it is definitely affecting my work and my home life. At work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that those pipeline reviews I mentioned just run really tense. It takes a lot out of me. And then by the time I get home, I'm just... I'm too drained and reactive to be fully present with my husband. So even if I'm not officially canceling plans or calling in sick, it's definitely taking a toll on how I function every day.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a situation where you feel completely at ease and your anxiety is completely gone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can't really remember the last time I felt completely at ease, to be honest. It's just always kind of humming in the background, even if it's a quiet day. So, no, it never really goes away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would improve if your sleep issues were resolved?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I mean... I'm sure sleeping better would help me feel less tired, but I don't know if it would actually stop the worrying. The anxiety feels like it's just part of me now, so I'm not sure if just fixing my sleep would make it go away completely.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F51.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um, I'm mainly here because my sleep has gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nIt's just... even when I go to bed completely exhausted, I just lie there replaying sales forecasts and team issues from work. And then I'll usually wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and I just can't get my brain to power down again after that.\n\nDoctor: How much are these sleep issues and thoughts about work affecting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely starting to affect things. Both at work and at home, really. I mean, at work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that our pipeline reviews end up running pretty tense lately. And then... by the time I finally get home, I'm just completely drained. I end up being really reactive, and I'm just not fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or mental images that you can't control, outside of just worrying about work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't get stuck on specific images or anything like that. It's really just... the worrying. Like my mind is just racing with things I need to do or things that could go wrong, mostly with my team or the sales numbers. But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I mean, I definitely feel run down and tired all the time from not sleeping, but I haven't noticed any actual physical pain or sickness like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about work or sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with work or your sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, work is definitely a big part of it, but my mind just kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Most days, I'll start out stressing over whether we'll hit our launch numbers or if someone on my team is about to quit. But then it just shifts right over to personal things, like paying the mortgage, and then back to a client renewal. \n\nIt just bounces around all day. It honestly feels like every small delay, no matter what it's about, could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're alone with no specific tasks or responsibilities to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Even when I have absolutely nothing on my plate, my brain just doesn't stop. I can literally tell myself, \"Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go.\" But... I don't know, five minutes later I'm right back in another loop. It's like I check one concern off in my head, only to have three more pop up. I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety always related to something specific that's happening or might happen, or do you ever feel anxious without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It's usually tied to something. I mean, there's always a reason I can point to, even if it's just a bunch of small things piling up. I don't really just sit there feeling anxious out of nowhere... my mind is always working on a specific problem or worrying about a scenario that might happen. It's just that there are so many of them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety seem to follow any kind of pattern or cycle throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a specific pattern, to be honest. It doesn't really cycle or peak at a certain time... it's just kind of always there. Like I mentioned, it's just this constant background noise from the time I wake up until I go to bed. So, no, I wouldn't say it follows any sort of schedule.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it seem to come on without any trigger at all?\n\nPatient: It's definitely triggered by specific things, but there are just so many of them. Most days, my mind just jumps from one issue to the next. Like, I'll start out worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers, and then I'm suddenly stressing about whether someone on my sales team is about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like the mortgage, or back to work with a client renewal. \n\nIt just feels like... I don't know, like every small delay or hiccup could turn into something major. So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related issues or does it also cover a wide range of other areas of your life equally?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just work. Work is a big part of it, sure, because there's so much going on there, but it spills over into everything else, too. \n\nLike I said, I'll worry about the mortgage, or if my car makes a weird noise I start stressing about how much it'll cost to fix and if we can afford it. I worry about my family, my health... just everyday things. It really just jumps around to whatever is going on in my life at the time. So, yeah, it covers a pretty wide range.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel your anxiety is significantly less than in others?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really. I wish I could say there was some part of my life where I could just turn it off, but it kind of bleeds into everything. Even when I'm supposed to be relaxing on the weekend or doing something fun, my brain is usually still going over some list of things I need to handle or worrying about what's coming up next. So, no, I haven't really found any area where it's noticeably less.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is something that you experience in response to specific situations, or is it more of a general feeling that you have all the time, regardless of what's going on?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more of a general feeling that just stays with me all the time. I mean, I feel wound up almost constantly. It's like... it's like I'm sitting in a stressful pipeline review at work, but I'm actually just at home eating dinner. And physically, my shoulders just stay tight no matter how much I try to relax. So even if there isn't a specific crisis happening right in front of me, that tense feeling is still just there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off from work or cancel plans due to feeling overwhelmed by your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I haven't actually taken a full day off work, no. But... it is definitely affecting my work and my home life. At work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that those pipeline reviews I mentioned just run really tense. It takes a lot out of me. And then by the time I get home, I'm just... I'm too drained and reactive to be fully present with my husband. So even if I'm not officially canceling plans or calling in sick, it's definitely taking a toll on how I function every day.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a situation where you feel completely at ease and your anxiety is completely gone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can't really remember the last time I felt completely at ease, to be honest. It's just always kind of humming in the background, even if it's a quiet day. So, no, it never really goes away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would improve if your sleep issues were resolved?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I mean... I'm sure sleeping better would help me feel less tired, but I don't know if it would actually stop the worrying. The anxiety feels like it's just part of me now, so I'm not sure if just fixing my sleep would make it go away completely.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety typically focused on a specific worry or concern, or is it more of a general feeling of apprehension that isn't focused on anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more general. It jumps around a lot. Most days, my mind just goes from worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers at work, to whether someone on my team is unhappy and about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like paying the mortgage, or worrying about a client renewal. It just feels like every single small delay or issue could turn into something major. It's not just one thing, it's a bit of everything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um, I'm mainly here because my sleep has gotten really unreliable lately. \n\nIt's just... even when I go to bed completely exhausted, I just lie there replaying sales forecasts and team issues from work. And then I'll usually wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and I just can't get my brain to power down again after that.\n\nDoctor: How much are these sleep issues and thoughts about work affecting your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely starting to affect things. Both at work and at home, really. I mean, at work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that our pipeline reviews end up running pretty tense lately. And then... by the time I finally get home, I'm just completely drained. I end up being really reactive, and I'm just not fully present with my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts or mental images that you can't control, outside of just worrying about work?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I don't get stuck on specific images or anything like that. It's really just... the worrying. Like my mind is just racing with things I need to do or things that could go wrong, mostly with my team or the sales numbers. But I wouldn't say I have those kinds of uncontrollable mental pictures you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to do or places you used to go that you're avoiding now because of your worries or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I still go to work, I still do the grocery shopping, things like that. I don't think I'm avoiding any specific places or activities. It's more just that when I'm doing those things, I'm constantly stressed and running through all my worries in my head. But I haven't stopped going anywhere because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health, such as headaches or stomach problems, since your sleep issues started?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I haven't had any stomach problems, and... no, no headaches either. I mean, I definitely feel run down and tired all the time from not sleeping, but I haven't noticed any actual physical pain or sickness like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about work or sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I feel wound up pretty much all the time. It's like... even when I'm just sitting at home having dinner, I feel this tension, almost like I'm sitting in the middle of a stressful pipeline review. My shoulders just stay tight, and honestly, no matter how much I try to relax or unwind, the physical tension is just always there.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel anxious and it has nothing to do with work or your sales forecasts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I mean, work is definitely a big part of it, but my mind just kind of jumps from one thing to the next. Most days, I'll start out stressing over whether we'll hit our launch numbers or if someone on my team is about to quit. But then it just shifts right over to personal things, like paying the mortgage, and then back to a client renewal. \n\nIt just bounces around all day. It honestly feels like every small delay, no matter what it's about, could turn into something major.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're alone with no specific tasks or responsibilities to worry about?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Even when I have absolutely nothing on my plate, my brain just doesn't stop. I can literally tell myself, \"Brenda, you've done the plan, let it go.\" But... I don't know, five minutes later I'm right back in another loop. It's like I check one concern off in my head, only to have three more pop up. I just can't seem to control it, even when I'm just sitting by myself.\n\nDoctor: Is there any part of your daily life where you feel completely anxiety-free?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I honestly can't think of a time when I feel completely free of it. Even when I try to just watch TV or read a book, it's always kind of humming in the background. I just don't get a break from it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety always related to something specific that's happening or might happen, or do you ever feel anxious without any clear reason?\n\nPatient: It's usually tied to something. I mean, there's always a reason I can point to, even if it's just a bunch of small things piling up. I don't really just sit there feeling anxious out of nowhere... my mind is always working on a specific problem or worrying about a scenario that might happen. It's just that there are so many of them, you know?\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety seem to follow any kind of pattern or cycle throughout the day?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed a specific pattern, to be honest. It doesn't really cycle or peak at a certain time... it's just kind of always there. Like I mentioned, it's just this constant background noise from the time I wake up until I go to bed. So, no, I wouldn't say it follows any sort of schedule.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it seem to come on without any trigger at all?\n\nPatient: It's definitely triggered by specific things, but there are just so many of them. Most days, my mind just jumps from one issue to the next. Like, I'll start out worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers, and then I'm suddenly stressing about whether someone on my sales team is about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like the mortgage, or back to work with a client renewal. \n\nIt just feels like... I don't know, like every small delay or hiccup could turn into something major. So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related issues or does it also cover a wide range of other areas of your life equally?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just work. Work is a big part of it, sure, because there's so much going on there, but it spills over into everything else, too. \n\nLike I said, I'll worry about the mortgage, or if my car makes a weird noise I start stressing about how much it'll cost to fix and if we can afford it. I worry about my family, my health... just everyday things. It really just jumps around to whatever is going on in my life at the time. So, yeah, it covers a pretty wide range.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel your anxiety is significantly less than in others?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really. I wish I could say there was some part of my life where I could just turn it off, but it kind of bleeds into everything. Even when I'm supposed to be relaxing on the weekend or doing something fun, my brain is usually still going over some list of things I need to handle or worrying about what's coming up next. So, no, I haven't really found any area where it's noticeably less.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is something that you experience in response to specific situations, or is it more of a general feeling that you have all the time, regardless of what's going on?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more of a general feeling that just stays with me all the time. I mean, I feel wound up almost constantly. It's like... it's like I'm sitting in a stressful pipeline review at work, but I'm actually just at home eating dinner. And physically, my shoulders just stay tight no matter how much I try to relax. So even if there isn't a specific crisis happening right in front of me, that tense feeling is still just there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off from work or cancel plans due to feeling overwhelmed by your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I haven't actually taken a full day off work, no. But... it is definitely affecting my work and my home life. At work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that those pipeline reviews I mentioned just run really tense. It takes a lot out of me. And then by the time I get home, I'm just... I'm too drained and reactive to be fully present with my husband. So even if I'm not officially canceling plans or calling in sick, it's definitely taking a toll on how I function every day.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a situation where you feel completely at ease and your anxiety is completely gone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can't really remember the last time I felt completely at ease, to be honest. It's just always kind of humming in the background, even if it's a quiet day. So, no, it never really goes away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would improve if your sleep issues were resolved?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I mean... So it's not really coming out of nowhere, it's just that almost everything I'm responsible for feels like a trigger.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related issues or does it also cover a wide range of other areas of your life equally?\n\nPatient: It's definitely not just work. Work is a big part of it, sure, because there's so much going on there, but it spills over into everything else, too. \n\nLike I said, I'll worry about the mortgage, or if my car makes a weird noise I start stressing about how much it'll cost to fix and if we can afford it. I worry about my family, my health... just everyday things. It really just jumps around to whatever is going on in my life at the time. So, yeah, it covers a pretty wide range.\n\nDoctor: Is there any area of your life where you feel your anxiety is significantly less than in others?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really. I wish I could say there was some part of my life where I could just turn it off, but it kind of bleeds into everything. Even when I'm supposed to be relaxing on the weekend or doing something fun, my brain is usually still going over some list of things I need to handle or worrying about what's coming up next. So, no, I haven't really found any area where it's noticeably less.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your anxiety is something that you experience in response to specific situations, or is it more of a general feeling that you have all the time, regardless of what's going on?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more of a general feeling that just stays with me all the time. I mean, I feel wound up almost constantly. It's like... it's like I'm sitting in a stressful pipeline review at work, but I'm actually just at home eating dinner. And physically, my shoulders just stay tight no matter how much I try to relax. So even if there isn't a specific crisis happening right in front of me, that tense feeling is still just there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off from work or cancel plans due to feeling overwhelmed by your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I haven't actually taken a full day off work, no. But... it is definitely affecting my work and my home life. At work, I spend so much time second-guessing my decisions that those pipeline reviews I mentioned just run really tense. It takes a lot out of me. And then by the time I get home, I'm just... I'm too drained and reactive to be fully present with my husband. So even if I'm not officially canceling plans or calling in sick, it's definitely taking a toll on how I function every day.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a situation where you feel completely at ease and your anxiety is completely gone?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I can't really remember the last time I felt completely at ease, to be honest. It's just always kind of humming in the background, even if it's a quiet day. So, no, it never really goes away completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would improve if your sleep issues were resolved?\n\nPatient: I don't really know, honestly. I mean... I'm sure sleeping better would help me feel less tired, but I don't know if it would actually stop the worrying. The anxiety feels like it's just part of me now, so I'm not sure if just fixing my sleep would make it go away completely.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety typically focused on a specific worry or concern, or is it more of a general feeling of apprehension that isn't focused on anything in particular?\n\nPatient: It's definitely more general. It jumps around a lot. Most days, my mind just goes from worrying about whether we'll hit our launch numbers at work, to whether someone on my team is unhappy and about to quit. And then it shifts to personal stuff, like paying the mortgage, or worrying about a client renewal. It just feels like every single small delay or issue could turn into something major. It's not just one thing, it's a bit of everything.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a specific stressful event or change in your life that you think might have triggered the start of your anxiety and sleep issues?\n\nPatient: Well, it wasn't just one sudden thing, but... I'd say it really started building up around the time we had a major reorganization in the sales division. That was about thirteen months ago. Things just got a lot more intense then, and it feels like the worrying and the trouble sleeping just kind of grew from there."
